Holding the seas' 28% at one level then takes 0.89 million km³ more water than the atlas's 10.7 million at first and about 1.1 million by ten million years, and the warm case 1.4–2.6 million ([geography](../geography/README.md#the-crusts-response-to-the-water)). The three overflows run 4,780 km through 35 hollows they fill, with 97 km of steep reaches in 27 places and drops of up to 654 m between neighbouring 1.9 km cells. Full, the three seas cover 1.69 million km² against 0.42 million at the common level, Moscoviense's 837,000 km² lake over the Freundlich–Sharonov lowland among them, so they drown land as well as making new shores; the riparian estimate (row below) measures the balance. No site in the heritage register lies under them or their overflow lakes. With the near-side ocean, the south sea and Humboldtianum at the common level, the water then covers 31.45% of the Moon against the atlas's 28.0%, and the upper seas hold 2.13 million km³ more; how that sits with the seas' 28% and where the water comes from were asked the same day. |

Full, Moscoviense passes on 47,800 m³/s on the closed budget and 55,800 on the routing's own. On the redrawn floor (domain/geography, `5f199f86`), full, Moscoviense passes on 47,800–55,800 m³/s and the salt sea held at the common level takes 28,400 and 15,600 of it, so 19,400–40,200 m³/s needs another way. The one modest gravity way out of any full sea is a ridge cut on Moscoviense's northern shore, 438 m deep at its deepest over 40 km (35.0° N, 139.7° E today), past which a new river runs about 2,000 km through 22 lakes across the dry northern far side to Humboldtianum; two more cuts of 89 and 56 m can take part of it to Smythii–Marginis and through its overflow to the near-side ocean. The division stays open. The division was chosen the same day (rows below). |

Holding Humboldtianum at the common level takes 6,200–7,500 m³/s against its own rivers' 0–250 m³/s; less holds it lower. Under the present temperatures the creep grows by about the same amount every tenfold of time and does not settle within the geology lane's billion years; that lane takes its present thermal state to describe the Moon for about 100 million years, after which the cooling interior stiffens the mantle and keeps the sag already made. The central and stiff profiles at a billion years bound what the present temperatures could add, and the warm mantle settles within about 10,000 years. At that state the Moon's turn is 6.15° (central), 7.49° (stiff) and 1.89° (warm), where the climate runs use the elastic 8°; the round's design-climate run takes the settled turn (next row). Computed on 16 November on domain/geography (`research/studies/atlas_round/`, 5cf5e850): at the settled state the common level stands 39 m above the atlas's, the South Pole–Aitken Sea is 540 m deeper at its deepest with its Tenebrarum sill 368 m down, and the whole takes 3.08 million km³ of water beyond the atlas's 13.37 million, most of the change coming within years from the water's own gravity. |
| The atlas round's design-climate run on the final surface takes the Moon's settled turn, 6.15° under the central profile 100 million years after the water arrives, in place of the elastic 8° of the climate runs so far, so that the climate and the surface stand at the same epoch. | Author, 2026-11-16 ("Very well. (a) it is."), on that recommendation against keeping the 8° | Recorded. The turned frame and surface at 6.15° are built before the run, which follows the drainage's rebuild on the redrawn floor; the run's 3–7 hours are unchanged. The 8° stays the first millennium's orientation (5 November). Found again on the final water (domain/geography `0a529baa`), the redrawn seas with the upper seas full and the governed lakes, with the geology lane's own method, which reproduces its 6.146° from its own load: the settled turn is 6.97°, the pole leaning toward 150.8° W and the point under Earth at 6.1° N, 0.8° E, with 8.28° (stiff) and 2.19° (soft) as bounds. The run takes the 6.97°, chosen the same day (rows below). |
| Bringing the surface close to its settled state much sooner and holding it there is considered soon. | Author, 2026-11-16 ("Note: Should it be too hard if we can do what we're doing already to get close to the settled state much faster and then hold it? That's something to consider soon too.") | Noted for the round. The crust's creep itself cannot be hastened: the settled sag within a millennium would need a load about five to a hundred times the seas' own. The visible surface can be built near the settled state and held while the crust creeps beneath, by water delivered to match, outlets or raised sills setting the upper seas' levels and the straits cut or built to their settled depths. The redrawn surface gives the gaps from the first years to the settled state: 2.4 m in the common level, 25 m in Moscoviense's rim, 9 and 6.5 m in Orientale's and Smythii–Marginis's, 0.29 million km³ of water, 55 m at the Tenebrarum sill and 17 m at the Crisium passage. The drainage step sizes the outlets. Sized on 16 November (`5f199f86`, with a read-only look on the research drive): each upper sea's pass is the same cell in the first years as at the settled state, so a sill 24.7 m high at Moscoviense's pass and cuts 9.2 m deep at Orientale's and 6.5 m deep at Smythii–Marginis's hold the settled levels from the start, each carrying that sea's overflow. The sill and cuts were chosen the same day (rows below). |

The criteria were set the same day from that check (domain/geography `b3d9a0a5`, `research/studies/atlas_round/satoyama.md`), and the first candidate map was made on the redrawn floor's drainage (`5f199f86`): 50,600 side valleys of 10–100 km², 1.57 million km², a third of the hybrid fifth and nearly all within 30° of the turned equator, could carry 204,000 km² of flooded fields giving 0.89–1.51 Tg of methane a year, within the room at the removal ledger's low-cost end and four times it at the high-cost end. The salt sea has 2,600 km² less than 10 m deep along 37,800 km of shore. |
| The satoyama-like landscapes' methods are chosen to limit methane buildup. | Author, 2026-11-16 ("Minro note as we work: We'll need to choose methods that limits methane buildup") | Recorded for the satoyama work, in line with methane held at 1.9 ppm by biological means with technology making up the difference (12 and 13 October). The literature check adds the methane of paddies, ponds and wetlands and the measured cuts by practice. |
| Methane-eating microbes are boosted to cut methane at its sources. | Author, 2026-11-16 ("We're probably going to want to try to boost methane crunching microbes.") | Direction recorded. It extends the biological means by which methane is held (12 October) and the aquatic programme's methanotrophs (19 October). The satoyama criteria count it at the paddies' oxic surface and roots, in the ponds and at the outlets (`research/studies/atlas_round/satoyama.md` on domain/geography); how far engineered methanotrophs can raise the cut is for the ecology lane to size. |
| Much of the satoyama-like activity lies in the hybrid land, under extensive management that limits methane and draws the most lasting output from it. | Author, 2026-11-16 ("We can also greatly reduce methane through managed activities in the hybrid zones. Hybrid land is the managed land beside the wild, megaparks and great gardens with food gardens among them, kept by heavy automation (11 and 12 October), and satoyama-like mosaics there take its garden-like, intensively managed form. Under the air space weathering ends and the iron rusts; the upkeep's dust of 1–42 mm a century renews the top metre every 2,400–100,000 years, so the soils take Earth's moderately weathered dry colours (Munsell): light brown 7.5YR 6/4 to yellowish red 5YR 5/6 on the highland rock, whose 4–6% FeO matches Earth's upper continental crust, and reddish brown 5YR 4/4 to the dark red of terra roxa 2.5YR 3/6 on basalt; the drylands' chosen soils and living crusts wait for the biome design. Of the 268,824 m³/s the rivers carry into the seas, 3,382 m³/s has gathered since the last lake, so plumes lie mostly in lakes and the neck's spill runs clear. | The globe has a control that peels away the air from the top down, by hand or with the zoom, so that the world below shows with less and less of the haze, as if descending through the atmosphere. | Author, 2026-11-17 ("I should comment that it might be nice to peel away layers of the air so we can see the world below with varying degree of obstruction as if we were desecnding through the atmosphere.Not sure when the best time to do that is.", then "Okay." to building it after version 13 and before the close views' tiles; on the peeled views, "Hmm... that's defnitely one option. And it's a good one. Though I'm not sure about the gloss and clear spotight. But the other thing I'm thinking is automatic peel of atmosphere on zoom. Kind of like flying into the atmosphere."; then "1. (a)" to the zoom standing in for the descent, offered at about 30 minutes against a camera that descends into the air itself) | Recorded; on domain/geography (`653637ac`; the globe page's version 15). The Air in view control's stops run from the whole air, 484 km deep, through 250, 150, 100, 75, 50, 30, 20, 10, 5, 2 and 1 km to the ground. The view looks through the air below the chosen height only, so the haze, the halo and a cloud deck above that height leave it, while every place keeps the light the whole air gives it, the decks' shade on the ground included. With the air's 48 km scale height, the air below 100 km holds 87% of the column, below 30 km 46% and below 10 km 19%. With the haze gone, the sunglint (the Sun mirrored in the sea, reddened as the Sun is by the air) and the sky mirrored in the water near the limb show at full strength; both are physical for a camera at that height, and the glint's spread rests on one guessed roughness for every sea (GGX α 0.2, about Cox and Munk's slopes for a 7 m/s wind). Since version 16 (`0b0094c2`) the peel follows the zoom by default (With the zoom): from the opening view to the nearest zoom the share of the air's column left in view falls from all of it to none, evenly with the logarithm of the camera's height, while the camera stays above the air; moving the slider takes the peel off the zoom. A camera that descended into the air itself would clear the haze only in the last few tens of kilometres, where even the close views' ground is soft. The glint follows the next row. |
| Place names on the Open Moon come from the Moon's own tongues, never Latin, English or mainland European; each region takes a tongue its landscape suggests, loosely as Earth's peoples sit in like landscapes, and the project makes the names. | Author, 2026-11-17 ("For naming... Try to avoid Latin and mainland European names for now."; on English descriptive names, "HUH?! NO! NO ENGLISH."; then Terlunan roots chosen; "Um I don't have a word list. You have the general construction.  Make it up."; "Overall, YOU need to make the name list."; "Remember to give some to other languages, including proto olivolingvo."; "I actually shuold map regions out with their own languages. I mean we can kind of see that some regions map a bit to various groups on Earth. There's plenty of Levantine/North African/Arabian arid, plenty of lush lands, lots of pacific island like regions. You get the idea.") | Recorded; research/studies/atlas_round/district_names.py on domain/geography (`c9c1e541`) names the draft's regions, districts and coastal seas in eight tongues: Terlunan (the author's conlang, v0.6) for shores, islands and the seven seas; Proto-Olivolingvo (the author's Esperanto–Hebrew tongue) for the polar lands and the semi-arid uplands; and six built for the atlas from everyday landscape words of their roots, without sacred terms: Sahran (Arabic and Amazigh) for the deserts, Tagalog-Hebrew (the author's idea) for the lowland rainforests, Pwani (Swahili) for the humid woodlands, Dala (Turkic and Mongolic) for the steppe, Runa (Quechua) for the high mountains and Satogo (Japanese) for the hill country. A name joins a head chosen by a place's centre to a modifier for what sets it apart. The Terlunan words the atlas needs beyond the v0.6 dictionary are coined in its manner and listed as proposals. Some names match places on Earth. All are working labels, for the people who come to live there to keep or replace. |
| The sea's glint and the sky it mirrors on the globe follow the design climate's surface winds, through the illumination domain's model of a wind-roughened sea at lunar gravity. | Author, 2026-11-17 (on the peeled views, "Though I'm not sure about the gloss and clear spotight."; then "2. (iii)" to setting each sea's roughness from the design climate's surface winds, offered at about 1–2 hours with a new field from the climate run, against keeping the fixed glint and sheen or a tickbox to turn them off) | Recorded; on domain/geography (`1685553c` the climate domain's surface wind product, `d966e705` the open sea's slopes in illumination/water_surface/open_sea.py, `0b0094c2` the page; the globe page's version 16). On the design climate (A28S at sunlight 0.930, years 35–49) the open water's median daytime friction velocity is 0.094 m/s and its median 10 m wind 2.37 m/s. The open seas' mean square slope comes out 0.0244, 0.017–0.030 across them, an rms tilt of 9°, against the fixed 0.04 drawn before: the short waves of Elfouhaily et al. (1997) above 1 m, and below them a fully developed sea's longer waves, about a quarter steeper than the sea appearance study's wave runs at its coasts' median winds. Over the climate run's land cells, which hold the lakes and the seas' edges, the wind 100 m up, where land and water share it, comes down over the water. The glint takes Gaussian facet slopes as Cox and Munk measured, Fresnel's law and Smith's shadowing, and the sky the water mirrors a table of rough water's reflectance of an even sky: seen 3° above the horizon it falls from level water's 0.73 to 0.42. On the near side at the ground stop the glint's core comes out narrower and brighter (its peak from 102 to 141 display levels) and the brightest sheen at the limb dimmer (164 to 119). The run's 3-day means leave out gusts and calm hours, which would break the glitter into patches, and the slopes are isotropic. |
| The design surface's falls are found and the globe marks the largest, with white water on steep reaches and spray round the biggest falls; the largest drops get profiles from LOLA's 59 m heights. | Author, 2026-11-17 ("Do we have info on any large waterfalls at this scale or maybe not zoomed in enough yet?", then "Both." to the marks with white water and to the finer profiles), offered at about 1 hour and 1–2 hours with a download of a few hundred MB | Recorded; on domain/geography (`baa0c2e2`, falls.py; the globe page's version 14). The design surface holds 858 reaches on rivers of 100 m³/s or more that fall 30 m a km or more; 146 carry more than 1 GW of falling water and 118 drop more than 500 m. The largest lie on the river that carries Moscoviense's overflow over its natural pass to the salt sea, 28,324 m³/s falling 3,449 m in 542 km, as a staircase down the Aitken crater's northern rim: 901 m in 4.5 km beside Aitken Z (42 GW) and 846 m in 10.2 km into Aitken (40 GW). The near-side neck's river is no fall: 95 m in 49 km. On LOLA's and SELENE's 59 m heights the six largest are long cascades down crater walls, no step steeper than 45°, their steepest 60 m 16–28°; the most fall-like, beside Aitken Z, runs 802 m down a wall averaging 24.5° over 1.76 km. Sheer faces of a few tens of metres would not show at 59 m. The white water and spray are display guesstimates. |
| The works' cuts are cut into the design surface's ground, so that the topography and the globe show the trench through Moscoviense's northern ridge with the branches' river in it. | Author, 2026-11-17 ("As importantly I'm looking at the topography view. Does it not have the cuts?", then "Go ahead."; on version 14, "Anyway I don't see the cuts on the topography."; then "3. (a)" to the canal line), on the cuts living only in the routing, with the trench drawn about as wide as a channel for its flow needs, a width the works have not designed | Recorded; on domain/geography (`baa0c2e2`, design_cuts.py; the globe page's version 14). The trench takes 17 cells of 1.9 km, about 29 km through the ridge, 437.9 m deep at the divide at 35.0° N, 139.7° E today, the deepest cut the routes record, and carries 14,176 m³/s, Humboldtianum's 5,340 and Smythii–Marginis's 8,836 on the design climate's closed budget. It drains to its water the two small lakes perched in the ridge that it crosses, 35 km² of their beds drying. One cell wide at 1.9 km, the trench does not show in the globe's topography: its colours and contours average the heights over 7.6 km cells, where the trench lowers the deepest by 61 m beside neighbouring cells 350 m apart on average, its raised relief takes points 21 km apart, and its shading from the 1.9 km heights draws a line no steeper than the ridge's own slopes. A Works checkbox marks each of its three pieces at its deepest point, and since version 16 (`d2af6a80` the courses, `0b0094c2` the page) draws each cut's course as atlases draw canals, an amber line a few pixels wide at any zoom in both modes, from the courses the cuts product now keeps in the order the water takes them (21, 3 and 2 cells). The trench carved into the close views' 470 m heights, offered beside it, was not taken up. The trenches are a product of their own, so that the design routing, which the climate run on the design surface names by its hash, stays as it is. The works holding the upper seas at their levels (a 24.7 m sill and cuts of 9.2 m and 6.5 m) are smaller than the cells show. |
| The design climate is the climate run on the design surface at sunlight 0.936, years 45–59, carried by pattern scaling to the 300.31 K at which the run at 0.930 settled on the final surface: an estimate, in place of a run near 0.9335. The round's drainage, maps, districts and globe are rebuilt on it, and the near-side sea's southern part settles where that climate puts it. | Author, 2026-11-18 ("At this point don't we have enough data to pretty much quickly construct end climate estimates without having to do a full run around a new dimming? It's not like there isn't a margin of uncertainty anyway."; then "(a) Build the estimate and rebuild on it"; of the near-side sea's southern basins settling 59 m lower on it, "Fair."), on the estimate (about 1 hour of work and 2 hours of machine time) against a run at 0.9335 (about 9 hours, then the same rebuild) | Recorded; on domain/geography (`7e0ed385` climate/gcm/pattern_scaling.py, `e5e9f378` the rebuild, `7cf4baa0` and `aa147f15` the globe page's version 18, whose region and district edges are thinner on the author's word). Two pairs of runs, one on each surface, agree on the land's response per kelvin of global surface temperature (rain +1.8–1.9%, evaporation +2.8–3.0%, the ground 1.07–1.08 K) while their maps of change are mostly the noise of short windows, so each field moves uniformly over land and over water by the 0.69 K step (the run's years 45–59 average 301.00 K by the climatology's area mean), and the fields the pairs disagree on, cloud over land, cloud by hour, snow and sea ice, stay as the run has them. On the design surface's land the estimate is no drier than the run at 0.930: on one land mask rain is 4% higher and the rivers' runoff 7%, the gain on the land the earlier run held as water, and drylands take 57.0% of the run's land cells against 57.1%, with less hyper-arid land and more humid land in place of wet. The rivers bring the seas 7% less in the run's own pattern over the design surface, so the near-side sea's main part pours 7,630 m³/s over the neck against 11,030, and the southern part settles at −225 m over 731,500 km², 59 m lower and 97,000 km² smaller; the strait at the neck stays on record as the alternative. The maps and the globe stand in the design surface's own settled turn, a degree from the final surface's. The draft districts are drawn again, 547 on land in 42 regions and 150 coastal seas, 499 of their 737 names standing. A run at the sunlight that holds 300.31 K on the design surface, about 0.9335, waits for the shield's dimming to be designed. |

I think (2) is fine as it is."), on candidates for each sea in its shores' tongues and on whether Terlunan should keep every region that is mostly shore | Recorded; on domain/geography (`e84eb1a7` the anchors, names table and calques, `1e4b805a` the page's rename without a rebuild, `39e3bcd1` the seas' names; the globe page's versions 19 and 20). 42 units carry an old name's meaning in their tongue, such as Marinosiyenloko, the calm lakes, on Mare Tranquillitatis, and Halij Asifa, the bay of storms, on Oceanus Procellarum; names after people, probes and Earth's places carry none and are not used. A rename edits research/studies/atlas_round/place_names.json, renames the record and the built page in seconds and republishes the page alone. |
| The globe's region and district borders run on across water as dashed lines, solid on land, and stay thin: for drawing only, the sea within about 100 km of land belongs to the nearest region, so each land border crosses straits, inlets and island chains and stops where the open sea begins. | Author, 2026-11-18 ("I get that we don't draw the heavy lines over waters, but it's causing an issue where we just have bits and scrap of lines in some areas and much of that region doesn't show reasonable divides. Maybe a dashed line or something there?"; on version 17's thicker lines, "Even that version shows tons of bits and piece scraps. It's the way you draw boundaries over water."; then "Proceed. Also keep the thinner line width now that you are aware the cause was the way you drew lines."), on giving the water within about 100 km of land to the nearest region and dashing the borders across it, offered at about an hour and a 3-minute page rebuild | Recorded; on domain/geography (`04d50641`; the globe page's version 21). A border was drawn only where land met land, so where land is broken by water, as round the Moscoviense sea's arms and islands, it survived only as scraps. Now the sea within 100 km of a shore carries the region of its nearest shore cell, each edge is solid on land and dashed over water (lakes included) in the same colour, and a coastal sea's divides from its neighbours are dashed too; no line runs along a coast or along the open sea's edge, where the coastal seas' outer limit had drawn a ragged line. The lines keep their thin widths, about 1.3 pixels for regions and 1 for districts. Superseded the same day: the 100 km rule drew a web of dashes round islets of one region lying off another's coast, and the regions now hold territorial waters instead (next row). |
| Each region holds its territorial waters, the coastal seas within 60 km of its shore, so its outline is closed; the sea beyond is international waters and belongs to no region; an island belongs to the waters it lies in, and island groups out in international waters are regions of their own, exempt from the regions' minimum. The globe draws the borders as smooth traced lines, solid on land and dashed at sea. | Author, 2026-11-18 ("Unbroken? That looks unbroken to you or are we seeinng different things?"; "Wouldn't it make more sense to create these as regions/districts/zones too instead? Actually... use an equivalent of "international waters" to get closed loop regions/districts. And island groups outside that get their own regions/districts. That might impact those islets."; "We should have clearly separated regions. Also... the lines are kind of too jagged. We need smoother divisions."; then "Yes." to territorial waters 60 km wide, the coastal seas' band, against a wider band for regions; and "Yep." to exempting island groups from the minimum and writing the change into the districts rules), after the full correction of the borders ("Proceed with the full correction.") | Recorded; on domain/geography (`0cfb0b4e` the territories and waters, `8c6adf46` the traced borders; the globe page's version 22). Each coastal sea belongs to the region of the land it faces and joins only its own region's water. The Moon's land is one mainland with 1,245 islands in its waters, each taking the region of the nearest shore, a chain of islands joining with its first link; three island groups of 3,000 km² or more lie in international waters and are regions, named in Terlunan (Raamania, 65,000 km² of land; 'Ute'utepuke; Abnupuehu), and 33 rocks smaller than that join the nearest district and claim no waters. The draft holds 45 regions, 548 districts on land and 155 coastal seas; a redraw keeps every generated name whose anchor and tongue survive, so 676 of 755 names carried over. The borders are traced once in the build, smoothed over about a dozen kilometres, and drawn as soft lines about 1.4 and 1 pixels wide at any zoom, closed at sea by the waters' outer limit, with even dashes measured along the line; no line runs along a coast. Districts rule 12 and the new rule 15 hold the rules. |
| The atlas globe is a full-window globe with floating panels: a click opens the card of the region, district, coastal sea or sea under the pointer; pin mode is its own tool, turned on by a button and off with Esc; a mouse over a place shows its name, what the name means and its text's first sentence; and each land region carries a short text written by hand from its computed fact sheet, describing its land, water, climate and sky, with its figures and place names filled in from the current record and the text listed as stale when a redraw moves a fact its wording rests on. | Author, 2026-11-18 (on tooltips describing the regions, choosing texts written for each of the 45 regions from its fact sheet: "Absolutely, and paying attention to style requirements. The interface is getting sloppy."; and "(A)" for the full-window globe with floating panels over tidying the two columns; noted for later, not this rework: "let's plan soon for a more Google Earth like feel to this where you can have your own projects, etc. Though I know that's going to take a while to code so for now that's just a note."), on a plan with a sample text for Kohulim | Recorded; on domain/geography (`03cb3ccb` the regions' fact sheets, `a87b9140` the 45 texts, `c1e95dff` the interface; the globe page's version 23). The fact sheets (research/studies/atlas_round/region_profiles.py, about 20 seconds) gather each region's place and Earth's height in its sky, land, heights, lakes, largest river, steep reaches, coastal waters, tides, heritage sites on land and under water, climate on the moisture map and neighbours, and how far its climate is read from (two island groups and the far side's lake countries take a shore's). The texts were drafted from the sheets at a lower setting and each reviewed and edited; they leave out people, settlements and vegetation, which await the humanities work and the biome map. The seas' water takes the seas' atlas names on the page, and the older Latin sea labels leave it wherever the regions are drawn. A new text, a rename or a change to the viewer republishes the page alone (render.py --names-only with --profiles). |

That'll save us time if we have to change things rather than generate all zooms first."), on the scans report and a plan in stages | Recorded; on domain/geography (`aed2520c` the 474 m surface, its tiles, the page's loader and the light; `d152f73e` the sustained rivers; the globe page's versions 24 and 25). The 474 m surface takes its shape from SLDEM2015 within 60° and LOLA's LDEM_64 beyond, keeps each 1.9 km cell's mean within 0.19 m, and puts water over 37.75% of the Moon against 37.16% at 1.9 km (islets raised, coves flooded). Its 873 tiles in 45 packs, 113.6 MB, load only within 3 Moon radii of the centre, so the opening download is unchanged; the ground's colour and the rivers stay at 1.9 km for now. Of 885 rivers carrying 100 m³/s or more, 607 keep that flow in every one of the design climate's fifteen settled years without storage, and 708 or 757 with the lakes' and upper seas' storage over wide or narrow outlets (an assumption: no outlet is designed); the drylands' losses take 30–309 m³/s. The study found 113 m³/s between the design routing's supply to the near-side sea's main part and the neck study's, open until the neck's balance is run again. The regions for the look's trial are proposed and await the author. |

On that state the seas hold, Bultengiz stands higher and larger, the lakes cover 18.0% of the Moon and two named rivers end in lakes; whether the design takes the rule through the chain is open. | Author, 2026-11-18 (of three ways to go on from the measure, to set the seas, the weirs and the evaporation factor again in the study first, to carry the rule through the whole chain at once, or to keep the present design and smooth the stepped shores close up, the author chose the first: "Alright option (1)") | Recorded; on domain/geography (`9718fb46` the corrected routing made in steps one floor can share; `82fcf65a` lake_rule_balance.py, its record and the README's section). Each setting is made by the procedure that made it on the present rule. The factor that closes the budget with every sea at the common level is 1.026 where the design's is 1.077, since the corrected lakes lose more of the rain to the air before it reaches a sea; one step leaves the corrected rule's budget 1,775 m³/s over and the present rule's 1,687. The weir at Hanaqucha's pass releases 24,315 m³/s to H'Jamo Melaĥa (26,599 in the design), the northern cut 7,850 (5,604), of which 6,164 reach Juldyztengiz after larger lakes on the way and are all that sea receives, and the branch to Dagat na Katzeh 12,113 (10,007). Uamoana stays over the neck's crest at 67.05 m below the common level and sends 7,365 m³/s over the neck (7,630); Bultengiz settles at 179.96 m below the common level where the design has 225.36, 78,376 km² larger; the other five seas keep their levels and lose less at the smaller factor. The lakes off the seas cover 6.83 million km² (18.0% of the Moon) against 5.10 million (13.4%) and hold 6.34 million km³ against 4.76; the land that is neither sea nor lake falls from 62.8% to 58.1% of the Moon; the state holds 1.62 million km³ more water than the design's. H'Naharo Longa still ends in the lake at 35° S, 88° E, now 291,323 km², and reaches Uamoana with 992 of 9,565 m³/s; H'Naharo Atika's 1,275 m³/s end in lakes; Hatunmayu carries 9% less because its weir releases less; the other waterways stay within 8% of their design flows or gain. Found on the way: near_side_neck.py's search for a part's level runs through the cells one by one and can stop part of the way through a basin that joins at one level; the study takes such cells together, the design's own levels come back within 2 cm, and the module is unchanged until it is next run. Limits: the climate was run on the design's surface, and the factor closes the books on the same rain, so a third more lake would change evaporation and rain in ways only a climate run on such a surface shows; were open water to lose as at the design's factor, the seas would be short as the measure found; the state is a steady one that the largest hollows would not reach by their own inflow; Bultengiz's level follows the small difference between Uamoana's rivers and loss. Open with the author: whether the design takes the corrected rule through the chain. |
| The design takes the corrected lake rule through its chain, and its climate is estimated on the surface its own rain fills from the runs already made, with no new climate run. The regions' draft of 18 November is kept until the author decides on a redraw. | Author, 2026-11-18 (of three ways on from the rebalance in the study, to carry the rule through the chain, to run the climate first on a surface with the larger lakes, or to keep the earlier design: "Definitely not (2). Again we've done so many runs that you can guestimate. You did it before.So (1) + guestimate climate."; when the first estimate, carried by the model's all-or-nothing mask, swung between two states of the lakes: "It's not likely that the climate does occiliate like that in those regions right?") | Recorded; on domain/geography (`26583e83` climate.gcm.surface_change; `9dc26ed1` design_works.py, hollow_courses.py, design_mask.py; `b7cfcde7` design_surface_climate.py and the routing chain; `d92caecf` the maps and waterways; the globe page's version 29). The climate: where the run on the final surface and the run on the design surface hold a cell differently (222 cells), the cell rains about a third as much as water as it does as land and evaporates at about the open water's rate, so a cell that turns to lake rains less and the land rains more. The estimate moves each cell by the change of its water fraction and is settled with the routing in seven rounds: water over 41.5% of the model's grid for the run's 37.5%, the land's rain 10% higher, the mean rain 1.958 mm/day for 1.902. Carried cell by whole cell the lakes and the rain chased each other between 15% and 18% of the Moon, an effect of the stepping and the mask; lakes fill over centuries and rain follows within a season, so the real feedback steadies them. The design: the factor that closes the budget is 1.043 (1.077); the weir releases 26,816 m³/s to H'Jamo Melaĥa, the northern cut 7,736, of which 6,237 reach Juldyztengiz, and the branch to Dagat na Katzeh 14,653. The near-side sea is one water across its neck: Uamoana 65.21 m and Bultengiz 66.61 m below the common level (Bultengiz was 225.36 m below, fed by a river over the neck), 2 to 3.5 m of water over the crest and 9,364 m³/s through it; Bultengiz covers 973,034 km² for 731,500. Lakes cover 17.3% of the Moon for 13.4% and no lake stands above dry ground beside it; seas and lakes hold 1.34 million km³ more. Rivers lead through their lakes to their seas; 18 of 22 waterways carry pinned names, H'Naharo Longa and H'Naharo Atika now name reaches ending in lakes, Jarykgol, Mto Jani and Mto Pacha have no waterway of 1,000 m³/s left, and four new waterways wait for names. Kept on the earlier design: the regions, districts and their texts (a redraw moves 38 of 45 land regions' anchors, so their names; their borders round Bultengiz follow the earlier shore), the year-by-year rivers and river losses (their storage has to be made again on hollows; the waterways carry no lowest-year flow meanwhile), the dryland rivers' routes, and the frame (the settled turn under the new water is not found again). Limits: the estimate rests on one pair of settled runs and assumes the displaced rain falls evenly on the land; a steady state the largest hollows would not reach from their own rivers; the sea's joining across the neck rests on 2 m of water. Open with the author: whether to draw the regions again and how to carry their names; names for the four new rivers and the fate of the three without one; the year-by-year rivers on the corrected rule. |
| The year-by-year rivers stand on the corrected lake rule and the design's own climate, each lake routed as one body of the hollows that stand full together, and the waterways carry their lowest-year flows again. | Author, 2026-11-18 (the year-by-year rivers were among what the first way on carried to the corrected rule, taken with: "Definitely not (2). Again we've done so many runs that you can guestimate. You did it before.So (1) + guestimate climate."; of the rivers that keep flowing through the dry years, earlier the same day: "Agreed. I want those done so I can see them.") | Recorded; on domain/geography (`f4ea3ccb` sustained_rivers.py on the corrected rule's network, with its record; `86c05b51` the waterways' record and the READMEs; the globe page's version 30, a wrap of its data). The module makes the design routing again in memory and goes on only when every receiver and flow is the product's, so the design routing and the maps that read it are untouched. A lake is a body of hollows standing as one water: 160,807 bodies over 207,078 lowest points, 135,379 of them below their passes. Replayed with the design's own lake outflows the network gives the design's flows exactly, and the years' own procedure within 0.43 m³/s, inside the 1 m³/s the weirs are set to. Each of the fifteen settled years is routed without storage and with the lakes' and the upper seas' storage over narrow and wide outlets. Routing each year as a steady state of its own is dropped: on the corrected rule it moves the shores off the design's cells, and in the storage's years a lake below its pass now rises through wet years and spills. Of 895 major rivers (100 m³/s or more, 10,381 km on dry ground) 594 carry 100 m³/s through every year without storage, 704 with wide outlets and 772 with narrow ones (607, 708 and 757 of 885 on the earlier rule's design); 89 stop in their driest year without storage (47), and 8 and 3 with storage. The twelve largest rivers into the seas lose 6–24% in their driest year without storage and 2–18% with wide outlets: Ninamuawa carries 30,359 m³/s in the mean and 29,823 in its lowest year. The near-side sea is one sea in this design, so no river runs over the neck and both parts keep their levels; in the years' mean the sea gains about 2,080 m³/s, 1.4 cm a year, the surplus that routing the years one at a time brings. On the design's own climate the dryland losses take 20–200 m³/s from the rivers (30–309 on the earlier rule's design). Limits: fifteen years; the structure held fixed (a lake that reaches its pass sends its water on by the design's course, and each cut carries its design part of its branch's share); the outlets' widths assumed; the procedure for a parted near-side sea kept and not run on the corrected rule. Still open with the author: whether to draw the regions again and how to carry their names; names for the four new rivers and the fate of the three without one. |
| The globe's close views and its rivers are examined for what is wrong and made again: the close-up look is to hold up as a view of land at that scale, a river is to read as a river, and the ground's green is to follow how much of the ground its water covers, so that a region whose seeps are many but small does not read as green country. | Author, 2026-11-19 (on the close views of the page's version 30: "I tried zooming in. Either it's not working for me or it's just not impressive. Also the rivers on the maps at views... All I see is a jagged weird blue line. Poorly done. I think it's time to figure out what's wrong."; "And there's also the fact that your current visualization makes regions look very green if they have extensive but very spotty seeps, etc."; of the plan put in answer, whose one view in a browser would have taken most of the processor for about a minute: "Good except... there's no way to do half or a quarter of a processer for two or four minutes? I don't like choking the whole processor like that.") | Recorded; begun on domain/geography on 19 November. Found that day from the tiles themselves, without a browser: all 135 pack files are in the published page; the trial windows' colour is weak in itself, a dull olive ground with soft blotches a few kilometres across, near-black water and no river to be seen; the named rivers are one line of constant width along the routing's 1.9 km cells, unsmoothed and straight across the lakes, where the borders are smoothed; and the green follows rain over evaporative demand with every hollow greened, whatever share of the ground its seep covers, so that Erg Ahmar's window has a green dot in every dry crater floor. Whether a browser shows the packs is checked first, in a view held to a quarter of the processor. A first phase then makes the look again in two windows (Paqarinwayqu, Erg Ahmar) and one river (Ninamuawa) as before-and-after pictures for the author's verdict, before any cut of the whole Moon. |
| The regions and districts are drawn again on the design's present water, and their names and descriptions are made again with substance: the draft's names are bland and generic, its descriptions likewise, and the descriptions dwell on hydrology. | Author, 2026-11-19 ("We're going to have to redo regions, etc. anyway, so while we're at it... First a lot of the names are too bland and generic.There's on substance there. Same with the descriptions. Also a LOT of the descriptions just obsess over hydrology."; of the plan put in answer: "Good except...", the exception being the processor's load) | Recorded; on domain/geography. It settles the item left open on 18 November, whether to draw the regions again. Of the draft's 548 district names 523 came from the generator's 16 heads and 35 modifiers; each district's description is one computed sentence; and 73 of the 80 sentences in the regions' texts mention water, since the fact sheets they were written from hold little else. The plan: the regions drawn again on the corrected rule's water; the fact sheets widened with rock and landform, sky and light, living cover and heritage; and names each resting on a stated particular of its place, tried on six regions for the author's judgment before any list is made. |
| How the hybrid land works as gardens and resorts, whether the managed biomes lie in the land of full habitation or in the hybrid land, and the general distribution of the kinds of land over the Moon are worked out for the atlas. | Author, 2026-11-19 ("We have considered little about the hybrid zones esp. as how they were supposed to function as gardens, resorts, etc. We have to consider if the managed biomes are in the full habitation zones or the hyrid, and we need to figure out general distribution."; "So that's going to require I'd say two subagents and your coordination.") | Recorded as open; begun on domain/geography on 19 November. The register fixes what hybrid land is (11 and 12 October), its share beside the wild (11 October, 16 November) and Jura Island's layout (15 October); it holds no map of wild, hybrid and habitation land and no rule for the managed biomes. A first phase gathers what is fixed, sorts each kind of managed biome with its reason as a proposal, and draws a first distribution on the design surface in two or three variants, for the author's decisions. Two subagents carry this study and the globe's look under the lead's review; their briefs and reports are kept on the research drive (session-tools/agents/atlas_land_use and atlas_picture). |
| The kinds of ground are placed and drawn on the globe: pans on the floors of dry hollows, soil kinds by climate zone and position, fresh silt at river mouths and along river corridors, and sand seas, the last through a study of wind and sediment supply. | Author, 2026-11-19 (asking of the trial look: "Hmm do you have soils and sands and whatnot reasoanbly well represented? Or would that really not shot as a difference at these views?"; of the answer and its four additions: "Yeah that's what's been wrong. It still reads as barren Moon even with the shading." and "Let's work on all of them. You have up to two subagents to use to coordinate.") | Recorded; begun on domain/geography on 19 November. This is the author's verdict on the first phase's trial look, whose reports are kept with their transcripts on the research drive (`session-tools/agents/atlas_picture/report.md` for the close look, its light and the rivers; `session-tools/agents/atlas_land_use/report.md` for the land's use, whose paper is `research/studies/atlas_round/land_use.md`). What was found of the ground: the look gives bare ground one smooth colour from the rock's iron and titanium and the climate's dryness, with rock on steep ground; the iron and titanium are mapped at about 60 km, mare basalt is 2.22 million km², 10.0% of the 22.13 million km² of land, and nothing places the kinds that come as patches (sand, pans, stony pavement, dark clays, dark humus soils, fresh silt), which the soils study of 3 November names by climate zone and setting. Plants cover 87% and more of the ground in the two wet windows and hide the soil; in Erg Ahmar's window they cover 57%, and drylands are 56% of the land, so there one soil colour runs over the Moon's relief. Two subagents work under the lead's review, their briefs and reports on the research drive: one studies where wind gathers sand on the design surface, from the design run's winds, the supply of sand and what holds it (`session-tools/agents/atlas_sand`), open to the answer that little sand moves; one draws the kinds of ground in the close look from the soils study (`session-tools/agents/atlas_ground`). Pans, soil kinds and silt in the look are display inferences from the soils study and the look's own reckoning of gathered water; the sand map is the study's product. |
| One to three sand seas are made in the hyper-arid land, fed with the sand in the sediment that the terrain's upkeep dredges from the lakes and returns uphill, and the natural wind-blown sand of the wild land is left open, held only near ways, settlements and the waters it would foul. A siting study sets their places, sizes and sand. | Author, 2026-11-20 (asking whether the managed design is why no sand sea forms: "it might be nice to have some of the really arid reegions have some sand seas"; of the three options, none, the natural sand left open, or one to three made seas in the hyper-arid land with the natural sand left open: "Third option is fine. Let's work on that.") | Recorded; begun on domain/geography on 20 November. The sand study of 19 November (`research/studies/atlas_round/sand.md`) finds that the land itself keeps sand seas from forming in its first ten thousand years: hyper-arid land is 362,457 km², 1.6% of the land; about 24 km³ of durable sand reach the wind, where one sea of 10,000 km² twenty metres deep holds 200 km³; crater bowls and lakes trap sand, and plants hold the arid land. Withholding the crust's upkeep from sand gives 249,033 km² under sand against 168,749 km², with dunes on 21,307 km² against 13,342 km², and no sea. The lever is the terrain study's loop (13 October): the lakes' dredged sediment holds about 0.17 km³ of durable sand a year, a sea of 10,000 km² twenty metres deep in about 1,200 years at about 0.45 GW of haulage over 500 km, inside the upkeep's 0.2–11 GW. The crust kept over the open ground of the arid and semi-arid land is still the soils study's proposal of 3 November, which no row chooses. The siting study is to give, for each candidate, its size and depth by delivery time, whether the winds hold it together, the dunes' forms and spacing, the sand's colour, the haul and its power, and what it would bury or foul; its paper and results join the sand study's folder, and the globe's trial draws the seas chosen. |
| The globe's clicking on regions and districts is corrected by a subagent beside the sand seas' work: a click on a district lights stretches of coastal sea with it, the same stretch for different districts and only parts of a coast, which reads as a fault. | Author, 2026-11-20 ("On the side let's have a subagent work on region/domain clicking. It's giving weird coastal highlights attached to domains.Like the same coast will light up with different domain clicks, but only parts of it. It's weird.", with two pictures of the districts Rumipampa and Hatunllaqta selected) | Recorded; begun on 20 November, with the brief, the pictures and the report on the research drive (`session-tools/agents/atlas_click`). Found by reading before the work: the page tints the selected district together with up to seven units linked to it, which for a land district are the coastal seas it faces and those its rivers feed, so neighbouring districts light the same coastal seas and a sea fed through a river lights far from the district; the tint is read cell by cell from the districts' texture, so its edge runs in steps beside the traced borders; and the cut to seven links also shortens the card's list. The aim: a click lights the place clicked and nothing else, with an edge that reads as the place's edge. |
| The regions' rules change in three ways for the redraw: an island joins a region only within a set reach of its mainland, a large separate land stands alone as a region of its own, and the Moon's summit seeds a region. | Author, 2026-11-20 (of the three changes proposed, with a redraw's cost of one to two hours of work and about two minutes of machine time: "Reasonable.") | Recorded; the redraw begun on domain/geography the same day. The faults the changes answer were found on 19 November: in the redraw on the corrected rule's lakes one region held its land in 375 pieces up to 2,700 km from its mainland and another had 59% of its land in its largest piece with a second piece of 218,000 km² 750 km away, because an island joined a region through any island within 60 km without limit and any landmass under 250,000 km² counted as an island of its neighbour; and the summit, with the sky port and the largest city, lay where three regions met. The reach and the size from which a land stands alone are set in the redraw and shown with it; the draft of 18 November stays on the page until the redraw carries names. |
| The way the atlas's names are made is reworked: it leans on one descriptive feature at a time and on description in general, and names derived from existing places are to be considered among other kinds. How to do it without choosing every name by hand is open. | Author, 2026-11-20 (of the draft's names: "The sand study makes clear that a lot of the naming really was generic and poorly thought out. I think there are a lot of "Erg" names that aren't ergs and have none. Might need to do a bit of a rework on regions and domains in general unfortunately."; of the sample name Akal Aqbur: "That one works anyway... Even though it's highly descriptive. I guess that's typical though. Most names are really descriptive names. But I feel like part of the problem is that you're leaning either too much towards one descriptive feature or towards descriptive names in general. We should consider names that are derived from existing places as well, etc. Overall, the naming "engine" is weak and I guess because it's highly algorithmic. Not sure really how to fix that without manually selecting so many names.") | Recorded. An audit of the same day against the sand map and the dryness classes: the region Erg Ahmar holds 0.34% sand under 58% plant cover; of the 18 districts named for desert or sand 11 hold under 5% sand and none is a sand sea; "dry" names 11 districts, 10 of them in the wet and humid zones; 703 of the 755 names came from the generator's 16 heads and 35 modifiers, and 313 of the 548 districts carry a modifier tied to no measure. A way put to the author the same day and not yet chosen: each region's naming history made by hand (who named it, in which layers of time, with a few anchor names), most district names derived from those anchors, gazetteers of each tongue's lands as model and source, names written region by region with every landform word checked against the ground, and the way judged on one or two regions named in full before any list is made. Two questions wait on the author: whether names may be carried over from existing places on Earth, and whether old lunar names may survive as worn forms beside the calques. |
| Names may take the existing names in use and fold them into a tongue's sounds, as calques or as forms transformed towards the tongue's own phonetics; Terlunan is a base and need not dominate the Moon's names, and the Moon has several languages of its own. | Author, 2026-11-20 (on the two questions of that morning's naming row: "Also don't over obsess with the Moon tongue. It's a base, but it doesn't have to dominate the world and it's totally fine to take existing names we use and folding it into the phonetics of the tongue, etc. Calque, transforms into more native phenetics, etc. are fine too. That gives more diversity. Plus as I said there really would be multiple languages of the Moon itself, as suggested with the other samples.") | Recorded. It answers both questions left open in the row above: existing names may be carried over and old names may live on as worn or transformed forms beside the calques. It refines the direction of 17 November that names avoid Latin, English and mainland European forms: an existing name enters in the shape a lunar tongue gives it. The way of naming proposed in the row above is tried next on one or two regions of the redraw named in full, for judgment before any list is made. |
| Existing names include the names of other places, on Earth among them, since new places are named after old ones; places are also named for people, for animals and for all sorts of other things. | Author, 2026-11-20 (""Existing names" I should note can mean names we use for other places like... on Earth, because people would name new places after old ones, as they do all the time. Right? Then there are places named based on people, on other things, fauana, all sorts of things, etc.") | Recorded; it completes the row above. The naming trial mixes these kinds: names carried over from places on Earth and from the old lunar map in the shape a lunar tongue gives them, names for people and events, names from animals and plants, names derived from a neighbouring name, and descriptive names whose landform words hold on the ground. |
| Every place name passes through a phonetic transformation: it keeps its parent language's sounds to a degree and comes out in the Moon's established phonetic framework. Where the naming trial offered alternatives its recommended names stand, and a prototype of the transformation on the trial's sample comes first. | Author, 2026-11-20 (of the naming trial on two regions and its choices: "I'm going with your defaults in terms of selection. The problem is the phonetic transformation or lackthereof."; "I think it's okay but none of those really went through the phonetic transformation process. It should preserve their original parent phonetics to a degree while still being transformed through the lunarian phonetic framework that's sort of established."; "Give me that same sample with that transformation done. It should be a quick pass as a prototype, which can be improved on if I like the sample.") | Recorded. The recommended names were Kostengiz for the plain between the two near-side seas, Keruenkum for its made sand sea and Galtap for the summit's tower and its metropolis; the trial's names stay a sample until the author has seen their transformed forms. The framework is read here as the sound section of the Terlunan reference: five vowels, no sh, z or affricates, syllables that open and close on one consonant at most, stress on the syllable before the last. The prototype was made the same day on the research drive for the Kazakh-, Mongolian- and Quechua-rooted names, each parent keeping what the framework holds of it (the Kazakh ng and rounding harmony, the vowels Quechua lowers beside its uvular): 21 of the sample's 34 names change, as Kostengiz to Kostengis, Jylansay to Yilansai and Wallataqucha to Walatakosa. The rules for the other tongues and the names already pinned wait on the author's view of the prototype. |
